\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.94\linewidth]{../shared/results/live_storage_scaling/live_storage_scaling.pdf}
\caption{HOT, WARM, and COLD request p50 with p95--p50 upper whiskers. WARM
recovery is reliable, but synchronous promotion remains visibly expensive.}
\label{fig:live-storage-scaling}
\end{figure}

Across cohorts, WARM/HOT p50 ratios range from 1.64 to 2.15. This changes the
product interpretation: persistent native K/V is now a correctness-qualified
backing representation, but request-path promotion is not yet economically
qualified. Prefetch, overlap, and hot-set admission must remove that stall;
quantized COLD additionally requires a model-and-workload quality gate.

The runtime now implements that next step without giving model-array mutation
to background Python threads. An event-loop-owned coordinator deduplicates
in-flight promotions by logical key, admits completed objects to a bounded hot
set, and overlaps WARM reads with query-side work. Across five QASPER objects,
MLX requires a 500 ms lead for 5/5 ready-at-demand and a 0.983 median
demand/HOT ratio; SGLang reaches 5/5 by 350 ms and a 1.063 ratio. With no lead,
the corresponding ratios are 2.123 and 1.561. All 70 paired outputs are exact.
This demonstrates latency hiding under oracle lead, not a faster storage
codec or a calibrated production predictor.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.74\linewidth]{../shared/results/mac_engine_extension/async_warm_frontier.pdf}
\caption{Scheduler-owned WARM promotion can hide request-path stall when useful
lead is available. The dashed line is HOT latency.}
\label{fig:runtime-async-warm}
\end{figure}

A two-host SGLang storage control then separates transfer lead from placement.
Five 125-token native objects reside on one 48-GiB Mac and are requested by a
model-side coordinator on a second Mac. A 2,000-ms lead makes all 40 requests
ready at demand; shorter leads leave a measurable remote-transfer stall. At
concurrency 16 and 250-ms lead, affinity placement reduces shared-resource
reads from 57.4 to 14.4~MB and raises control-plane throughput from 12.24 to
28.38 requests/s. For mixed resources it reduces reads from 272.7 to 71.8~MB
and raises throughput from 2.25 to 11.77 requests/s. Tensor equality is exact.
This is a controlled native-K/V storage result over tunneled HTTP, not yet an
online SGLang generation or distributed-HiCache latency claim.

The same native executors now traverse the HTTP streaming gateway. SGLang
delivers 3.04--3.20 requests/s and MLX 1.56--1.66 requests/s over concurrency
one through eight. Tail latency grows with the intentionally serialized model
runners; cancellation after first token and cleanup succeed, with no retained
request-owned native state across 36 exercised sessions. The model locks are a
correctness boundary: an audit found that MLX cache captures were views into
reusable arrays. Owning evaluated K/V arrays at the capture boundary restores
HOT and WARM isolation and prevents later requests from mutating retained
memory.

A separate MLX finite-wave curve extends physical-tier concurrency to 16.
Every one of 124 HOT/WARM requests is baseline-exact. At concurrency 16, a
shared WARM resource requires one promotion, sustains 2.91 requests/s, and
avoids 630 MiB of duplicate K/V; 16 independent WARM resources require 16
promotions, read 1.26 GiB, and sustain 1.22 requests/s. This quantifies the
benefit of immutable physical sharing while also exposing the queue cost of an
unbatched, serialized runner.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.76\linewidth]{../shared/results/mac_engine_extension/storage_concurrency.pdf}
\caption{Shared and independent MLX resources through 16 concurrent sessions.
The finite waves include promotion and serialized model queueing.}
\label{fig:runtime-storage-concurrency}
\end{figure}

Long-session pressure adds 1,125 requests across QASPER, HotpotQA, and 2Wiki.
With an eight-resource cyclic working set, HOT capacities two and four average
17 reloads per seed and reload every final revisit; capacity eight has zero
reloads and evictions. Quality is invariant because selection is frozen. A
20-example QASPER selective-int8 study reaches only 4/20 exact generations
when all K/V is quantized; restricting quantization to the late quarter reaches
14/20, still below the lossless product gate despite small aggregate F1 change.

A further 1,000-request QASPER matrix crosses HOT/WARM resource budgets
$\{2,8\}$ with local rotating windows $\{64,256\}$. The small WARM budget
produces 24--75\% SOURCE starts, whereas WARM eight eliminates them. F1 is
identical in all eight cells and window size has no material completion-tail
effect. Full disk-backed WARM does not reduce resolve p95 relative to
re-encoding these bounded sources, making reload-cost-aware admission a runtime
requirement rather than an optional optimization.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.68\linewidth]{../shared/results/mac_engine_extension/long_session_pressure.pdf}
\caption{Sustained pressure exposes a working-set threshold rather than a
quality effect: once all eight resources fit, reloads disappear.}
\label{fig:runtime-long-pressure}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Interpretation}

The results separate three claims that are often collapsed. Selection can
improve evidence availability and reduce visible context before any engine
change. Native Memory can preserve the same selected evidence while moving it
outside prompt accounting. Native Serving becomes economically useful only
when the engine owns attachment, placement, reuse, and cleanup cheaply enough
to offset its control-path cost. Exactness and token reduction close broadly;
the economic gate remains engine- and workload-dependent.

\section{Cross-Model, Precision, and Product Qualification}

\subsection{Common Qualification Contract}

The qualification contract asks four separate attribution questions.  First,
No PRA versus Selected Context measures PRA routing and visible-context
construction.  Second, Selected Context versus Native Memory holds selected
record IDs and intervals fixed and isolates native-K/V realization.  Third,
Native Memory versus Native Serving holds selection and representation fixed
and measures scheduler-owned placement, reuse, and lifecycle.  Fourth,
no-adaptor versus bundle comparisons hold execution mode fixed and isolate the
exact model-specific bundle.  We use stable condition identifiers for all
seven mode/adaptor combinations.  In particular, E0 is Selected Context and is
already a PRA condition.

The available flagship engine cohorts primarily answer the second question.
True matched No-PRA arms for Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-14B, and
Llama-3.2-1B remain \texttt{NEEDS\_RUN}; exact bundle-versus-no-adaptor
end-task arms are also absent.  We therefore do not attribute the large E0/E2
visible-token difference to PRA versus ordinary inference.  It is only the
effect of realizing the same PRA-selected evidence as native memory.

All engine rows now answer one question: at fixed or acceptably changed
quality, when does PRA reduce total serving cost? The benchmark computes each
query, candidate set, selected IDs, and selected intervals once. A cryptographic
selection digest is then reused by \texttt{FULL}, \texttt{E0\_SELECTED},
\texttt{E2\_HOT}, and \texttt{E2\_WARM}; optional E3 conditions add prefetch,
remote WARM, COLD, or SOURCE restoration without rerunning retrieval. Context
size, shared versus independent resources, and concurrency 1, 4, 8, and 16 are
manifest dimensions. This separates retrieval quality from representation and
transport.

Every row pairs task quality with serving measurements. Binary workloads derive
successful requests per second as request rate times task success and report
successful tasks per accelerator-hour. Monetary cost remains null unless an
auditable hourly-cost source is supplied. Visible-token reduction and active-K/V
reduction remain separate. Tail percentiles, physical memory components,
transfer bytes and time, cache events, reuse, hardware metadata, status, and
provenance are first-class fields. The current legacy registry has no row with
both request rate and task success, so its quality-adjusted-throughput field is
honestly \texttt{NOT\_MEASURED} rather than reconstructed after the fact.

Figure~\ref{fig:engine-maturity} distinguishes a validated level from a deeper
observed mechanism. In particular, AirLLM has a live E2 mechanism smoke but
remains E0-qualified until natural quality and larger-than-VRAM evidence close
the gate; SGLang has local E3-like HiCache controls but lacks distributed,
scheduler-owned E3 validation. Raw throughput is deliberately absent from this
maturity axis.

The matrix now distinguishes companion evidence from generic provider defaults.
On vLLM-Metal, selected native pages recover 75/75 answers and reach 34.41
requests/s at concurrency eight; after the first request in a native-selection
namespace, APC supplies 128--144 prompt tokens. Alternating
selected and ordinary rows satisfy their expected outcome in 40/40 cases. On
SGLang-MLX, an 81-token shared-prefix hit composes with external HiCache:
selected A/C recover 10/10, cleanup B recovers 0/5, and every selected request
contains exactly one memory copy. A separate five-seed bridge uses SGLang's
built-in file-storage backend: fresh adapters hit and recover 5/5, with 38.7 ms
mean writes, 122.2 ms mean cold L1 restoration (36.2 ms median), and 0.013 ms
warm lookup. This is a local backend-contract result, not distributed HiCache.
On MLX-LM, routed ordinary and native K/V
agree on 60/60 original-answer QA pairs; evidence recall@4 ranges from 0.348 to
0.775, so the companion result retains an explicit open oracle gap. These are
pinned controlled realizations,
not portable claims that the default SDK provider may advertise unconditionally.

The shared matched benchmark makes the product boundary sharper. MLX-LM and
SGLang-MLX preserve all 840 E0 outputs while moving 90.6--93.0\% of visible
prompt tokens into native K/V. Their current selected-cache wrappers remain
12--16\% more expensive in reused or concurrent regimes. vLLM-Metal also
preserves 840/840 outputs with zero pairwise F1 change. Its four macro cost
ratios lie between 0.981 and 1.004. An earlier 593/840 result compared
full-prefix E0 prefill against query-only E2 prefill; a five-example audit shows
that APC-restored E0 and native E2 match 5/5 while full-prefix and APC-restored
E0 themselves differ in 2/5. The SDK therefore treats all three pinned paths
as semantic mechanism passes while keeping their economic status experimental.
Engine labels alone never select these capabilities.

For product evidence we normalize each run into explicit staged conditions
rather than exposing only paper-level E0/E2 labels: Selected Context (the
frozen PRA selection rendered as visible text), Native Memory without a learned
adaptor (the same selected source materialized as native K/V), and a
mode-matched condition for an exact learned-adaptor bundle.  No PRA is a
separate ordinary-inference condition and appears only when actually run.  The
qualification runner pins model revision, tokenizer, quantization, engine, and
selector output. It executes QASPER, HotpotQA, and 2Wiki separately and records
the source artifacts and checksums in the card. Thus a result for MLX 8-bit is
never inherited by MLX 6-bit, bitsandbytes 8-bit, or another checkpoint.

Table~\ref{tab:quantized-bundle-quality} preserves the superseded exact-identity
cohorts for auditability; it is not a current qualification table.
The MLX runs contain 20 examples per dataset and five dataset seeds (60 unique
questions per identity). The smaller CUDA bitsandbytes run contains five
examples per dataset (15 total) and is intentionally presented as controlled,
not production-scale, evidence. Native Memory is promoted only when exact
sequence agreement is at least 95\% and mean token-F1 change is no worse than
$-0.01$. This gate is stricter than average-score similarity: it rejects the
Gemma path despite its modest $-0.0036$ F1 change because only 3/60 sequences
match exactly.

\midrule

Gemma-3-1B MLX 8-bit & 60 & 3/60 & 0.0735 & 0.0699 & $-91.1\%$ & $+5.9\%$ & Candidate \\
Qwen3-4B MLX 8-bit & 60 & 60/60 & 0.1315 & 0.1315 & $-91.5\%$ & $+1.9\%$ & Promote \\

Historically, the passing Qwen and Llama rows preserved output exactly while hiding about
91.5\% of selected source tokens from the visible prompt. Median TTFT changes
are $-6.9\%$ for Llama, $-9.2\%$ for Qwen3-4B, $+0.7\%$ for Qwen3-8B 6-bit,
$-5.7\%$ for Qwen3-8B 8-bit, and $-2.4\%$ for Qwen3-14B 8-bit. Mean completion
overhead within the Qwen 8-bit family falls from 1.9\% at 4B to 0.6\% at 8B
and 0.3\% at 14B. This is a useful scaling observation, but not yet a scaling
law: the cohort and host are fixed and the models have different output
distributions.

The two rejected rows identify different integration boundaries. Gemma's mixed
local/global attention geometry needs architecture-specific native-memory
calibration. The eager bitsandbytes path preserves evidence recall and removes
91.7\% of visible tokens, but loses 0.3813 combined token F1 and takes roughly
three times as long end to end; Selected Context therefore remains its default.
Neither failure is converted into an unspecified missing value.

The public catalog applies the same schema to 17 local bundles. Each card
enumerates every declared engine, mode, and profile and labels absent work by
cause: \texttt{NEEDS\_RUN}, \texttt{NO\_QUALIFIED\_ADAPTER}, or
\texttt{CALIBRATION\_PENDING}. It preserves \texttt{NOT\_MEASURED} when no
stronger cause is established. The ten pre-gate native-runtime records are now
rendered as \texttt{QUARANTINED}/\texttt{NEEDS\_RUN}; they do not retain their
former promoted or candidate status. The generated site evidence matrix remains
the exhaustive current view.

These quarantined results do not show that a learned adaptor is necessary. The formerly promoted
BALANCED profiles use parameter-free routing and all eligible consumer layers.
The Qwen3-4B bundle includes an opt-in learned router whose QASPER recall gain
does not transfer to HotpotQA; it is not substituted for a missing end-task
adaptor arm. Other exact identities state \texttt{NO\_QUALIFIED\_ADAPTER}.
The machine-readable audit retains this distinction for cards, CLI reports,
and Control Plane views.

\subsection{Precision and quantization robustness}

Precision is now part of the canonical evidence key rather than an informal
model-name suffix.  Each row records a broad numerical family (for example,
\texttt{INT4}) and the concrete encoding (for example,
\texttt{MLX-4bit}); serving, feature-extraction, and adaptor-parameter
precisions remain separate.  This prevents evidence for MLX 4-bit weights from
silently qualifying AWQ, GPTQ, GGUF, BF16, or another conversion of the same
source model.  Converted artifacts additionally admit source and tokenizer
revisions, quantization configuration hashes, conversion recipes, and artifact
checksums.

The normalized ledger contains 53 planned or historical exact-precision rows.
Its pre-gate Native-Memory measurements are now quarantined; their cells must be
treated as rerun-required rather than as current observations.
Table~\ref{tab:precision-ladder} preserves the earlier rows for auditability,
while the machine-readable supplement retains all missing and blocked states.
The earlier Qwen3-14B cross-precision comparison is therefore diagnostic only
and does not currently qualify either encoding.

The public workflow is reproducible through
\texttt{pra model qualify-precision}.  It resolves an immutable model identity,
classifies staged memory gates, validates imported evidence against the exact
precision key, and emits the canonical JSON, CSV matrix, card fragment, and
LaTeX table from one record.  Every current local bundle now declares
\texttt{supported\_precisions}; the catalog exposes size, family, encoding,
engine, mode, profile, datasets, and evidence tier.  No learned-adaptor arm is
inferred from the presence of a structural bundle.

A matched M5 study isolates precision-specific MLX cost on ten fixed
MultiHop-RAG questions. Table~\ref{tab:precision-rag} reports the deployed
No-PRA and Native-Memory pipelines: one-shot native execution costs 5.8--6.1\%
more total latency for BF16, 10.0--10.7\% for INT8, and 14.8--16.1\% for INT4.
The row retains the quality delta only as a guardrail. Because this historical
cohort changed both selector and representation, it is not a selector-frozen
transport comparison; Papers~3.2 and~3.3 own its retrieval interpretation.
It also predates \EngineContract{}; all native
exactness and cost values in this study are historical diagnostics, not current
precision qualification.

The small, one-seed cohort is directional engine evidence. A selector-frozen
non-Qwen replication, Qwen3-14B BF16, and peak-memory measurement remain
\texttt{NEEDS\_RUN}; absent adaptor arms remain
\texttt{NO\_QUALIFIED\_ADAPTER}.

The historical pre-gate expanded confirmation raises each engine to 149 unique questions and
2,086 paired requests. Every engine is 2,086/2,086 exact, for 6,258/6,258 pairs
overall; the pooled Wilson 95\% lower bound is 0.9994. Visible-token reduction
is 90.4--93.0\%. The macro ratios in Table~\ref{tab:engine-matched-expanded}
show why semantic and economic gates remain separate: vLLM-Metal is within
0.9\% of parity in every regime, SGLang-MLX pays 5.4--7.4\% on reused paths,
and MLX-LM pays 7.2--13.9\% while using the same frozen evidence.
These values are retained for audit only and do not establish current engine
parity or economics.

The vLLM audit also establishes a product invariant that is easy to miss at an
HTTP boundary. APC hashes visible prompt state, but native PRA evidence is
hidden from that state. A native provider must therefore derive an opaque APC
salt from tenant scope, ordered logical-memory identity, selected-token count,
position policy, and consumer layers. Stable selections retain warm reuse;
changed selections cannot inherit query K/V produced under another memory.
The SDK exposes this derivation as an engine helper and tests equal-selection
reuse, changed-selection isolation, geometry sensitivity, and identifier
non-disclosure. A five-example capture/replay audit preserves E0's first token
in 5/5 cases at the nominal position base. Holding source-ingestion and
query-prefill geometry fixed raises complete 24-token parity from 3/5 to 5/5;
the full corrected cohort then reaches 840/840.

The product surface includes installable package metadata, Hugging Face loading,
runtime config serialization, direct and external reference registration,
session isolation, callable and folder-backed capability registration, lazy
view activation, typed resource discovery, safe execution, compact result
ingestion, selected replay, address search, cursors, size-adaptive native result
routing, lazy native-region promotion, generation, inspection, benchmark
commands, and two executed notebooks. The canonical \texttt{pra} CLI exposes
separate \texttt{model}, \texttt{adapter}, \texttt{profiles}, \texttt{bundle},
\texttt{runtime}, \texttt{agent}, \texttt{gateway}, and Hub-only \texttt{hf}
namespaces. \texttt{pra-hf} remains a deprecated alias for one release cycle.
The \texttt{pra gateway serve} command launches the independently testable
gateway with health, capability, logical-request, and OpenAI-compatible
endpoints. A deployment can therefore
discover unsupported paths before serving.

\subsection{Model onboarding, calibration, and community distribution}

The product workflow treats architecture adaptation, learned routing
adaptation, profile calibration, engine realization, and Hub distribution as
different operations. \texttt{ModelInspector} reads configuration metadata
without loading weights. \texttt{StructuralAdapterBuilder} emits a versioned
declarative mapping for conventional Qwen, Llama, and Gemma decoder attention.
\texttt{ModelValidator} records V0--V9 independently, so discovering Q/K/V
projections cannot be reported as semantic success. The later stages cover
disabled-PRA parity, native capture, visible-prefix equivalence, source-relative
position geometry, cached decode, grouped-query layout, selected-region
consumption, and generation.

\texttt{ProfileCalibrator} packages measured rows from the existing benchmark
registry. A small-cohort optimum remains \QualityMaxCandidate{} with
\textsc{Smoke}/\CalibrationPending{} evidence; only benchmark-scale validation
may assign \QualityMax{}. \texttt{PRAModelBundle} then combines immutable base
model identity, structural and learned adapters, profiles, evidence, runtime
compatibility, engine realizations, and provenance without copying base-model
weights. Hub pull and push are optional transport operations under
\texttt{pra hf}; generic Transformers execution does not depend on Hub login.
The public catalog currently contains nine canonical
\texttt{EInnovator/pra-*} bundles. In addition to Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-4B,
Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-14B, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Gemma-3-1B, it includes
matched general-instruction and code-instruction Qwen2.5-1.5B identities. A declarative
manifest orders the Collection by measured usefulness rather than publication
date: Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B lead, while the 0.6B artifact is explicitly a
research/reference bundle. Bundle discovery checks the base model,
revision, engine, and immutable bundle revision before AUTO may resolve it;
checksum validation precedes managed runtime loading. A release gate requires
one qualified recommended profile and rejects routing-only headlines,
unpaired absolute quality, profile conflicts, and revision or quantization
mismatches. The published learned routers remain dataset-qualified research
profiles rather than universal defaults.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.88\linewidth}\centering
HF model $\rightarrow$ inspect $\rightarrow$ structural adapter
$\rightarrow$ semantic validation $\rightarrow$ optional learned adapter\\[3pt]
$\rightarrow$ profile calibration $\rightarrow$ PRA bundle
$\rightarrow$ runtime/agent/engines $\rightarrow$ optional Hub publication
\end{minipage}}
\caption{The model-onboarding path. Each arrow is a separately inspectable
service boundary rather than an implicit side effect of one CLI callback.}
\label{fig:model-onboarding}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Engine-neutral runtime providers}

The canonical launch path is \texttt{pra runtime serve} with a model and
\texttt{-e ENGINE}. Click parses the request, \texttt{RuntimeManager} resolves a provider, and that
provider translates a common model, revision, PRA bundle/profile, device,
session, and cache contract to the upstream engine. Repeated
\texttt{--engine-arg KEY=VALUE} values preserve engine-specific escape hatches
without cloning upstream CLIs. Providers return serializable handles and
distinguish process start from readiness. Inspection reports static declarations;
doctor checks report installed dependencies and live endpoints separately.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.86\linewidth}\centering
\texttt{pra runtime serve} $\rightarrow$ \texttt{RuntimeProvider}
$\rightarrow$ \{HF, vLLM, SGLang, MLX\}\\[3pt]
agent $\rightarrow$ [optional gateway] $\rightarrow$ runtime $\rightarrow$ engine
\end{minipage}}
\caption{One runtime command preserves the separation between agent policy,
optional gateway mediation, and engine-native execution.}
\label{fig:runtime-provider}
\end{figure}

The default vLLM provider deliberately advertises E0 unless the measured native
bridge is installed. Paper 6 now establishes both a controlled Metal V1 path
and a version-bounded CUDA 0.28 worker hook with scheduler-invisible detached
pages. The CUDA cohort is exact at 128 requests per condition, but HOT and WARM
E2 trail E0 useful throughput and LMCache was not exercised. This is measured
native capability, not an unconditional production default.
SGLang and MLX advertise the companion papers' measured native mechanism
support under their pinned environments. Paper 6.1 additionally closes local
shared-prefix/HiCache composition at the runner boundary, while distributed
HiCache and E3 scheduler-aware PRA remain false. The shared
capability object feeds runtime inspection, gateway negotiation, agent launch,
and benchmark records.

\subsection{Agent-level configuration and interactive surfaces}

Low-level \texttt{pra.yaml} controls model/context execution. A separate,
versioned agent profile controls model endpoint, workspace, durable sessions,
tool approval, skill directories, MCP declarations, task policy, and generation
budgets. Resolution is explicit CLI options, selected named profile, default
profile, project config, user config, then package defaults. The short option
\texttt{-p} selects an agent profile; \texttt{-P} independently overrides PRA.
Credential files remain server-side references and are redacted from session,
inspection, benchmark, and model-card output.

Agent profiles now independently select
\path{context.transport=auto|pra|text}, text-fallback permission, and required
features. The CLI exposes \path{--context-transport} and
\path{--allow-text-fallback/--no-text-fallback}; \path{-P/--pra} continues to
select the model/runtime PRA profile and is not overloaded with wire semantics.
The launch summary, TUI \path{/runtime} command, and web session diagnostics
report requested and resolved transport, capability source, gateway mode,
integration level, resource-delta support, and native-K/V status without
including record bodies.

\texttt{pra agent chat} is the stable TUI reference and can resolve a default
profile without model flags. \texttt{pra agent run} provides a noninteractive
form. The optional FastAPI/WebSocket UI is another presentation layer over the
same \texttt{PRAAgent}, \texttt{AgentLauncher}, session, task, and authorization
services. It supports multiple durable conversations and sequence-based replay
after reconnect. A dangerous tool call remains blocked until the backend
receives a typed one-shot approval; the browser cannot bypass host policy. The
UI binds to loopback by default and is experimental rather than a scientific
contribution or production security claim.

The fleet Control Plane follows the same separation at deployment scope. REST,
streamable-HTTP or stdio MCP, the built-in Control Plane agent, and an embedded
operator CLI call one transport-neutral \texttt{ControlManager}. Its fleet,
Registry, deployment, action, qualification, observability, context, experiment,
and audit managers own authorization and policy; engine and Registry clients
remain behind backend protocols. A declarative operation catalog maps semantic
operation IDs to permissions, side effects, and risk, and drives REST route
exposure and MCP tool discovery. A disabled operation is therefore absent from
OpenAPI or MCP discovery rather than merely failing after dispatch. The default
MCP surface is disabled; when enabled it is read-only except for plan
construction, with apply and experiment submission separately denied.

Operational mutation uses a common plan--apply transaction. The plan records
current and projected state, impact, reversibility, restart requirement,
required permission, confirmation requirement, tenant, expiry, and idempotency
key. Plans and completed results are durable, so REST, MCP, and CLI retries share
the same idempotency decision. Manager-owned audit records add roles,
permission, request and trace IDs, transport, before/after state, and outcome.
An official MCP SDK protocol test initializes both stdio and authenticated
streamable HTTP, discovers the configured tools, and invokes the same fleet
manager used by REST. This is interface validation rather than a serving-quality
or latency claim.

\subsection{PRA under agentic coding workloads}

The coding-agent campaign uses end-task success as the admission gate and
context economy only as a secondary objective. Terminal-Bench 2.1 is delegated
to Harbor 0.22.0 with frozen task identifiers, fresh task containers, official
verifiers, and preserved agent-native context policy. An oracle first passed
the downloaded \texttt{filter-js-from-html} task, establishing that task setup
and grading were functional. OpenCode 1.18.26 with Ollama/Qwen3-14B then
completed all five No-PRA smoke trials without harness exceptions, but solved
0/5 tasks while passing 7/12 individual verifier tests and consuming 100,883
cumulative input tokens. We therefore did not promote that pairing into a PRA
profile sweep.

The follow-up campaign makes that sequencing executable rather than relying on
operator convention. Each published baseline pins source and model revisions,
harness, cohort, scaffold, engine, precision, context and step limits, decoding
policy, and official grader. A resumable scheduler writes a durable receipt
after every state transition. Gateway pass-through, Selected Context, and
native-PRA cells cannot launch until their exact No-PRA dependency is marked
\texttt{BASELINE\_REPRODUCED}. A smoke subset or a changed engine,
quantization, or context window remains
\texttt{BASELINE\_ATTEMPTED}; it can qualify infrastructure but cannot authorize
a PRA efficacy claim.

Gateway placement is not synonymous with PRA execution. We therefore factor
the promoted agent experiment into two binary ownership axes. The ordinary
controls are direct engine access and G00 pass-through to that same ordinary
engine. The native comparison is (i) an agent sending typed selected context
directly to a PRA-capable engine and (ii) the same agent using a G11 PRA
gateway in front of that engine. This defines two experiments that must not be
conflated. In \emph{G11-equivalence}, the complete request and selected-resource
envelope are replayed, and matching resource digests make the difference an
estimate of gateway mediation. The direct run writes an ordered selection
fixture keyed by a digest of the complete agent-visible request. Replay has no
ordinal or approximate fallback: a changed trajectory aborts the equivalence
cell instead of admitting a different selection. In \emph{G11-end-to-end}, each placement owns
its normal session and selection path; this estimates product behavior, with
selection and transport jointly present. Both fix the engine, model revision,
task and repeat, budget, profile, and official grader. Easy-50 contains both
the fail-closed frozen replay and a separate route-owned product cell. G10
remains a separate selected-text fallback and is not relabeled as native
execution.

Every Easy-50 arm fixes a 4,096-token per-turn completion ceiling in addition
to the 32,768-token context and 50-step limits. This ceiling is part of the
execution fingerprint. It bounds pathological continuations when a backend
does not emit its normal end marker; ordinary shorter actions are unchanged,
and a ceiling hit is retained as a protocol event rather than a PRA quality
outcome.

The executable version-2 matrix records connection, engine-PRA and gateway-PRA
flags, gateway mode, physical engine target, and comparison group in every row.
It refuses native labels unless endpoint preflight reports effective native
K/V, and it completes each direct task before launching its gateway mate.
Paired reports count outcome agreement, regressions, recoveries, token traffic,
and wall time. Mini-SWE-agent and Qwen Code are the first enabled external
harnesses; OpenCode, Pi, OpenHands, Aider, SWE-agent, and endpoint-configurable
commercial agents enter only after their own No-PRA baselines and protocols are
admitted. The first-party PRA Agent is deliberately last and runs both direct
and through G11. That pair serves two purposes: comparison with external agent
loops and diagnosis of PRA Agent session, tool, typed-record, or transport
faults when its outcomes diverge. Different agents remain separate experimental
units because their prompts and tools differ.

The 40-cell Terminal-Bench direct/G11 matrix is a transport qualification,
not the principal efficacy experiment. Its two enabled harnesses each resolved
only 1/10 No-PRA tasks, and both resolved the same \texttt{fix-git} identity.
At that floor, one outcome change dominates the relative rate. The matrix can
still reveal protocol failures, crashes, isolation errors, duplicate native
materialization, and accounting regressions. PRA efficacy is instead tested on
the admitted Easy-50 cohort described below, whose direct ordinary baseline
resolved 14/50 tasks. Its preregistered execution order is corrected G00,
direct native PRA at 50\%, frozen G11 equivalence, G11 end to end at 50\%,
matched 50\% truncation, and G10 at 50\%; lower budgets follow only when that
comparison is informative.

The first capable reproduction target is TIGER-Lab FIM-14B under its published
R2E-Gym configuration~\cite{wang2026fim,jain2025r2egym}: all 500 SWE-bench
Verified instances, an ordinary vLLM endpoint, BF16 weights, temperature zero,
a 65,536-token limit, 40 normal steps with an absolute cap of 100, and official
SWE-bench container grading. The
reported three-seed mean is 29.20\%. The available CUDA host has only 8~GiB of
device memory, so it cannot host this exact 14B cell. A quantized FIM-7B
llama.cpp run on the 48~GiB M4 is therefore recorded only as a No-PRA
distributed-path calibration. This distinction prevents a convenient but
non-identical local configuration from being mislabeled as reproduction.

The calibration completed two seed-42 instances through an ordinary
llama.cpp endpoint, the unmodified R2E-Gym scaffold, and official SWE-bench
container grading. It resolved \path{django__django-13821} and did not resolve
\path{sympy__sympy-19954}: 1/2, with a Wilson 95\% interval of
9.5--90.5\% and no grader errors. The resolved task terminated naturally after
31 model calls; the unresolved task reached the 40-step limit. Across both
tasks the agent made 72 calls, accumulated 1,123,772 prompt tokens and 11,878
completion tokens, and recorded 607.4 seconds of trajectory time. The small
cohort does establish a functioning distributed
model--agent--container-grader path and a nonzero task signal. It does not
estimate population accuracy: llama.cpp, Q4\_K\_M, two instances, and a
32,768-token context differ from the published
vLLM/BF16/500-instance/65,536-token setup. The scheduler therefore assigns
\texttt{BASELINE\_ATTEMPTED}, and no gateway or PRA cell is admissible yet.
The next fixed No-PRA cohort must contain enough tasks to estimate a nontrivial
success rate and stable trajectory costs before any treatment is considered.
Its receipts retain cumulative and maximum prompt tokens, a conservative
unique-context estimate, repeated-context fraction, calls, tools, trajectory
length, patch size, wall and model time, termination, and official grader
outcome. Retrospective normalization of the two trajectories finds 50,106
maximum-prompt tokens across tasks versus 1,123,772 cumulative prompt tokens,
a conservative 95.5\% repeated-context estimate under the accumulating
R2E-Gym scaffold. This load is a primary future treatment target, but savings
will count only beside a matched solved-task rate. TTFT and prefill remain
explicitly unmeasured when the endpoint does not expose them.

Increasing code-model capacity did not close this admission gate. OpenCode with
Ollama/Qwen3-Coder-30B scored 0/1 on
\texttt{query-optimize} and passed 4/6 verifier tests. It made five tool calls,
read the source query, and wrote a candidate, but retained a correlated
subquery. The official performance verifier consequently consumed almost its
full 30-minute allowance (1,791.2 seconds total task wall time). The run used
50,876 cumulative input tokens and had no harness exception. This distinguishes
a model-level optimization failure from gateway or tool-transport failure, but
still supplies no quality floor for comparing PRA profiles.

Holding the model, gateway, engine, and \texttt{query-optimize} task fixed
exposed substantial harness effects even before PRA was enabled.
Table~\ref{tab:coding-agent-gate} reports the one-task diagnostic. OpenCode and
Pi both read and wrote candidate SQL, but neither met exact-output and runtime
requirements. OpenHands entered a repeated-continuation loop and did not create
the requested file. The OpenHands row uses version 0.57.0 because Harbor's
adapter expects a module path absent from 1.11.0. These sparse rows qualify
execution paths; they are not an agent ranking.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\caption{Same-model No-PRA Terminal-Bench mechanism gate. Input tokens marked
N/R were not exposed by the pinned harness integration.}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrl}
\toprule
Agent & Success & Tests & Input tokens & Wall time (s) & Tool path \\
\midrule
OpenCode 1.18.26 & 0/1 & 5/6 & 20,996 & 466.0 & read + write \\
Pi 0.73.1 & 0/1 & 4/6 & 5,219 & 478.1 & read + write \\

We then expanded the stronger-model gate to a preregistered ten-task
Terminal-Bench 2.1 cohort. Qwen3-Coder-30B Q4\_K\_M served every trial through
Ollama on a 48~GiB M4 Pro, while Harbor and the official verifiers ran on a
separate 16~GiB Apple host. Mini-SWE-agent 2.4.6, Qwen Code 0.23.0, and Aider
0.86.2 each attempted the same ten tasks, for 30 completed No-PRA trials.
Mini-SWE-agent and Qwen Code each solved \texttt{fix-git}; Aider solved none.
Thus aggregate success was 2/30, or 6.7\% (Wilson 95\% interval
1.8--21.3\%), but only one of ten unique tasks was solved by any harness.
Table~\ref{tab:coding-agent-expanded-gate} reports the observed workload. These
rows are not an agent ranking because each harness retains its own prompt,
tools, and loop policy.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\caption{Expanded Qwen3-Coder-30B No-PRA admission gate. Token totals cover
only harness integrations that report usage; N/R means not reported, not zero.}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
Harness & Runs & Success & Input tokens & Tool calls & Wall (h) \\
\midrule
Mini-SWE-agent & 10 & 1/10 & 2,562,781 & 271 & 1.14 \\
Qwen Code & 10 & 1/10 & 4,154,315 & 143 & 1.84 \\
Aider & 10 & 0/10 & N/R & N/R & 1.04 \\
\midrule
Observed total & 30 & 2/30 & 6,717,096$^{\dagger}$ & 414$^{\dagger}$ & 4.02 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\par\vspace{2pt}\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Coverage is 20/30 trials because

\label{tab:coding-agent-expanded-gate}
\end{table}

Of the 28 unsuccessful trials, 21 completed with an official score below the
success threshold, six reached the agent timeout after usable model activity,
and one reached the verifier timeout. Retaining timeouts as outcomes avoids
silently improving the apparent success rate. The larger model and broader
harness set therefore establish substantial real coding-agent traffic---the 20
token-reporting trials alone accumulated 6.72 million input tokens---but not a
stable enough quality floor for a treatment comparison.

This negative gate matters to the product claim. Token reduction from a model
that no longer completes coding tasks is not a PRA benefit. A code-capable
agent/model pairing must first establish nonzero success and enter the
preregistered 20--80\% admission band, with 30--70\% preferred. The expanded
cohort's 6.7\% result falls below that lower boundary, so the scheduler correctly withheld treatment
launches. Only after admission may identical tasks compare
No PRA, PRA Selected Context without an adaptor, and Selected Context with the
immutable Runtime Bundle under
BALANCED. Table~\ref{tab:agent-canonical-gate} makes the unrun conditions
explicit: \texttt{BLOCKED} is missing evidence caused by the quality or
reproduction gate, not a synthetic zero or an observed PRA failure. The normalized schema retains
official score, verifier counts, turns, tool calls, cumulative context, TTFT
and ITL distributions, decode output tokens/s, model-call requests/s, queue and
inference time, resource use, and cost for that subsequent paired analysis.

\paragraph{Difficulty-qualified local admission.}
We next froze an easier cohort before inference by filtering SWE-bench Verified
revision \texttt{c104f84} to its \texttt{<15 min fix} human-difficulty label,
ordering eligible identifiers by a salted SHA-256 digest, and taking the first
20. The ordered cohort digest is \texttt{021b532a...3bd75fb}. This is a local
capability calibration, not a claim of reproducing the H100 study below.
Qwen3-Coder-30B Q4\_K\_M, served by Ollama 0.32.7 on an M4 Pro, used the
unmodified mini-swe-agent 2.4.6 backticks scaffold, a 32,768-token context,
50 steps, temperature zero, and the official SWE-bench 4.1.0 Docker grader.
The model resolved 9/20 tasks, or 45.0\% (Wilson 95\% interval
25.8--65.8\%). This falls inside the preregistered 30--70\% preferred band and
therefore admits the nested Easy-50 treatment frontier.

The 20 trajectories made 525 model/tool turns and accumulated 4,286,673 prompt
tokens and 84,145 completion tokens. Summing each task's maximum prompt gives
226,423 tokens; the remaining 4,060,250 tokens are a conservative 94.7\%
repeated-context estimate under the accumulating scaffold. Four submissions
had empty patches, two had patches rejected by the official grader, and two
reached the step limit. These failures remain in the denominator. This baseline
is already a materially stronger test than the floor-regime Terminal-Bench
cells: the agent is competent, while repeated context is large enough for
selection to matter. No PRA quality or savings conclusion is drawn from the
admission row alone; those require the identical Easy-50 tasks under
pass-through, matched truncation, and PRA Selected Context.

The nested Easy-50 No-PRA expansion resolved 14/50 tasks, or 28.0\% (Wilson
95\% interval 17.5--41.7\%), and retained one one-hour agent timeout as an
unresolved result. Thus the larger estimate contracts from the optimistic
9/20 calibration prefix but remains above the preregistered 20\% treatment
floor. Across 1,229 model/tool turns it accumulated 10,115,822 prompt tokens
and 200,677 completion tokens. The per-task maximum prompts sum to 526,997
tokens, leaving 9,588,825 tokens, or 94.8\%, as conservative repeated context.
Eight tasks produced empty submissions and 13 produced patches rejected by the
official grader; all remain in the denominator. The pass-through and
matched-budget conditions reuse these exact 50 identifiers and execution
settings.

An initial G00 pass-through attempt produced a nominal 0/50, but it is not a
quality observation. Every trajectory stopped on its first model call with an
\texttt{InternalServerError} and an empty submission. The downstream URL had
been supplied in the conventional OpenAI form ending in \texttt{/v1}, while the
adapter appended a second \texttt{/v1}; the resulting upstream HTTP 404 escaped
the request handler and appeared to the harness as a disconnected server. The
same implementation also discarded generation options and reconstructed a
reduced completion envelope. We therefore mark all 50 rows
\texttt{INVALID\_TRANSPORT} and exclude them from PRA deltas. The repaired G00
path normalizes both origin and \texttt{/v1} API roots, pins and advertises the
served model, preserves OpenAI request and response fields, returns structured
upstream errors, and uses an explicit upstream timeout. A benchmark run now
must pass health, mode, model-catalog, and live one-token generation probes
before its first task. The clean corrected parity gate then reran three tasks
previously solved by the direct baseline and resolved all 3/3 again with the
official grader, zero timeouts, and zero grader errors. It used 69 model calls,
403,703 prompt tokens, and 9,503 completion tokens, versus 63 calls, 458,690
prompt tokens, and 8,573 completion tokens in the corresponding direct
trajectories. This tiny selected-on-success diagnostic establishes end-task
transport parity, not an efficiency advantage or a population-level quality
estimate; the two runs followed different trajectories despite temperature
zero, and gateway latency was confounded by network placement and a shared
single-slot model server. It clears the transport gate for a fresh corrected
Easy-50 comparison.

\subsubsection{Claim 1: correct live-history semantics}

Native profile qualification exposed three agent-history correctness bugs.
First, mini-swe-agent can reject a malformed assistant action and append a
format-error user observation without retaining the rejected action; the live
KV sequence must therefore support exact-prefix backtracking rather than assume
append-only history. Second, independently selecting message fragments can
retain two assistant actions while omitting the intervening tool observation,
which Qwen's chat template rejects. Third, treating only the latest observation
as mandatory can still discard the assistant command that produced it. On the
task-01 trajectory this changed the second generated response and immediately
caused a format error. The corrected selector ranks historical atomic causal
turn bundles: one assistant action plus all following user/tool observations up
to the next action, while the complete active action--observation tail is
mandatory. The task statement remains pinned, selected bundles retain causal
presentation order, and the complete reconstructed request must pass the
physical model's chat template before resource materialization. Intermediate
frozen replay
of the admitted task-01 trajectory validated every one of its 31 user-ending
request prefixes at each of the 75\%, 50\%, and 25\% profiles (93 rendered
requests total). This validates template grammar, not causal completeness or
task efficacy; both fragment-level runs and profile attempts made before the
active-tail correction are excluded from solve-rate and efficiency deltas and
must be rerun.
The revised segmenter also treats message/tool records as the first physical
boundary. Short records remain intact; overlap never crosses records; and an
oversized observation splits at file headers, stack frames, test cases, or diff
hunks before falling back to token windows. Every child retains a stable parent
record and causal-group ID plus return-code, mutation, and result metadata.
Child slices are disjoint before budget accounting, while physical engine
packing preserves their logical identities. Once engine correctness qualifies,
the matched segmentation ablation will compare fixed token windows,
record-aligned messages, causal bundles, and hierarchical causal bundles with
selective regions inside oversized observations.
The engine path also stopped deleting the pinned selected-history sequence when
its digest changes. It submitted the replacement text to the same owned slot
and let llama.cpp retain the exact common token prefix while trimming and
re-evaluating the divergent tail. A physical M4 probe cached 2,450 of 2,455
replacement-prefix tokens and evaluated five tokens versus 2,455 under cold
materialization. This is useful composite-prefix reuse, but it does
\emph{not} qualify agent-history PRA: any selected text after the common prefix
is encoded again, and several selected records are collapsed into one physical
resource. We therefore reclassify this path as a rematerialization diagnostic.

The first corrected active-tail efficacy result separates those layers. On
task 01, both the same-engine plain control and the 100\% live-prefix PRA arm
resolve the task in 31 model calls with exact trajectories. The 75\% selector
matches them through two generated actions, then diverges when it drops the
oldest completed turn on request 3; it submits after 23 model calls but its
nonempty one-line patch fails to apply, for 0/1 officially resolved. Estimated
logical-to-physical context falls by 23.2\%. Independently, resource-delta
telemetry records 83,644 selected-prefix token-exposures but only 33,600 newly
evaluated resource tokens, with 43,852 exact common-prefix cache hits, three
identity hits, 18 delta updates, and no physical K/V copy. These counters
describe ordinary longest-common-prefix reuse of changing selected text, not
arbitrary selected-record K/V reuse. This selector profile also fails its
task-quality gate. The row is retained only as a diagnostic and does not admit
an end-to-end gateway or PRA efficiency claim. The task-01
50\% arm also resolves 0/1: it reaches the 50-step limit with no patch while
reducing estimated logical context by 47.0\%. Its 167,908 resource-managed
selected-prefix token-exposures require 57,301 newly evaluated tokens; 83,775
tokens hit exact delta prefixes, 13 requests are identity hits, 34 are delta
updates, and no K/V copy occurs. The 25\% arm likewise resolves 0/1 at the
50-step limit with no patch. Its pinned active tail limits realized estimated
savings to 67.6\%, but delta/identity reuse reduces newly evaluated resource
tokens to 19,193 across 116,572 resource token-exposures. A frozen-selection
cache on/off factorial was planned, but the Easy-50 matrix is now stopped until
the live-prefix subset implementation passes the correctness gate below.

An initial frozen factorial appeared to fail a stricter consumption gate. The
cache-off 50\% anchor submitted an unresolved nonempty patch after 44 model
calls and 507.9~s. The cache-on arm replayed the same selected records and
logical requests for three calls, but its third response differed and request
4 consequently had no matching frozen digest. A subsequent 100\% audit found
that this was not yet a pure prefix-cache factorial: cache-on continued the
live full-prefix slot, whereas cache-off routed the lossless selected history
through detached resource materialization. We therefore retain the run only as
an implementation diagnostic and exclude its partial score and runtime ratio.

The repaired adapter treats 100\% retention as a semantic no-op. If no live
slot is consumed, it validates and reconstructs the complete ordinary chat
prompt and cold-prefills that prompt directly; it does not enter detached
resource materialization. With llama.cpp revision \texttt{458681e1}, batch 256,
microbatch 1, and Qwen3-Coder-30B Q4\_K\_M, a frozen 30-turn trajectory then
matched cache-on and cache-off on all 30 continuations while all 29 eligible
cache-on requests reported physical hits. More importantly, the autonomous
task-01 control now matches on all 24 logical-request hashes, ordered-selection
digests, and complete responses. Both arms emit the same 689-byte, 14-line
patch and resolve under the official grader. Cache-on retains 120,848 of
126,772 physical prompt tokens (95.3\%) and completes the agent in 100.3~s,
versus 2,792.5~s cache-off, a 27.85$\times$ single-task speedup. This is a
qualified strict \emph{prefix-cache versus cold-prefill} control, not a PRA
speedup: 100\% retention selects no subset and the cache-on arm remains on its
ordinary live slot.

\paragraph{Agent-history K/V correctness reset.}
We stopped the Easy-50 treatment matrix after recognizing that the composite
resource design violated the product invariant. For an agent trajectory, PRA
must select cells already encoded in the session's live prefix cache; it must
not tokenize and prefill the selected transcript again. The replacement
llama.cpp path names one pinned live source sequence plus multiple stable
record ranges. Unified-cache \texttt{seq\_cp} adds request membership to those
existing cells without copying K/V, preserves their original positions, and
removes request membership without deleting source membership. Newly evaluated
query and generation cells are committed back to the source. The final sampled
token, which llama.cpp returns before evaluating it into K/V, is explicitly
reported as pending and bridged in the next suffix.

On an M4 Pro with Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct Q4\_K\_M, a 20-turn same-state gate
forked the control and treatment from identical source K/V on every turn. All
20 continuations matched token-for-token as the source grew from 11 to 343
tokens; zero selected tokens were re-encoded and every suffix commit succeeded.
The record-aware agent wrapper then exercised a genuinely sparse turn: from a
531-token live source it attached 26 K/V tokens (4.90\%), evaluated none of the
selected history text, copied no K/V, and returned the pinned key correctly.
A separate rejected-action path removed the discarded branch by rolling the
source from 27 to its 23-token common prefix, then committed the recovery suffix
with zero selected-text re-encoding and returned the correct recovery answer.

The first current-build real-agent gate uses predeclared baseline-success task
02, \texttt{django\_\_django-\allowbreak15368}; task 01 was not substituted after both its
matched current plain and selected-K/V executions became 0/1 under the revised
binary.  The task-02 ordinary-prefix-cache control and the 100\% live-K/V arm
both resolve 1/1 in 14 model calls, emit the same 673-byte, 13-line patch, and
match on all 14 assistant responses byte-for-byte.  After the initializing
request, the live arm continues the canonical same-slot sequence on every turn:
54,686 resident K/V token-exposures are reused, only 2,996 wire-suffix tokens
are evaluated, selected-history text is never re-encoded, and no physical K/V
copy occurs.  Wall time is 141.4~s for the plain control and 184.1~s for the
instrumented live arm, so this row establishes correctness, not speedup.  An
earlier replay of task 01 also matched both controls through 19 responses, then
failed closed because an \texttt{ls -la} observation carried different file
timestamps and therefore a different exact request hash.  That is a frozen-
observation harness defect, not a model-response divergence; replay fixtures
must freeze tool observations or key them by stable causal identity.

At 90\% budget the same task passes through four exact full-retention actions.
The first sparse request attaches 3,047 of 3,221 resident source tokens and
drops the oldest completed causal turn while retaining the task, active tail,
and two most recent turns.  Response 5 changes in wording but issues the same
command; the subsequent trajectory edits the intended line, then submits an
invalid 503-byte patch after 12 calls and resolves 0/1.  The run realizes only
10.2\% estimated logical-context saving.  This is the first measured sparse
trajectory failure on the corrected K/V mechanism, but it is not yet
attributed solely to selection.  In the frozen consumption control, live K/V
and fresh prefill match exactly on the four full-retention requests.  At the
first sparse request, compact fresh prefill consumes 3,543 tokens while the
live-K/V request spans 3,717 logical positions: the 174 omitted tokens leave
positional holes because the native path deliberately preserves original
record coordinates.  The responses differ slightly in reasoning text but
issue the same command.  Thus ``identical selected records'' is insufficient
to isolate K/V construction unless positional presentation is fixed too.

A position-preserving fresh-evaluation control now closes that narrower
correctness question.  It cold-prefills the frozen 3,221-token source, attaches
the identical 3,047 selected K/V entries to a clean request sequence at their
original positions, and evaluates the same 496-token wire suffix.  Its response
is byte-for-byte identical to the live-K/V response at the first sparse request
(matching SHA-256
\texttt{f9b4fceb64011a3c79a0c607f8e0b5efec23e3a32c89ea0d9db6d66b8cd45cdf}).
Consequently, the native path is reusing the intended K/V subset without
selected-text re-encoding or K/V-construction drift.  The earlier compact-fresh
difference is a positional-consumption effect.  The task-level 0/1 result can
therefore be assigned to the sparse treatment as a whole, but selection loss
and original-position versus compact-position consumption remain separate
factors for the next frozen factorial.

The paired stochastic control strengthens this single-seed result.  After an
exact replay of the four source-building turns, five predeclared sampling seeds
(101, 202, 303, 404, and 505; temperature 0.7; 128-token continuation cap) fork
from the same 3,047 selected cells.  Each live-built continuation is
byte-for-byte identical to its position-preserving cold-built counterpart;
the five seeds produce five distinct response hashes, and all five pairs match.
This clears the multi-seed live-versus-fresh K/V-construction gate for this
model, request, and physical slot geometry.  It does not establish quality at
90\% retention, because both arms consume the same sparse state.

Cross-model replication exposes a necessary qualification dimension.  With
Qwen3-14B Q4 and its default model template, turn 2 fails closed before
inference because the template inserts an empty thinking wrapper when an
assistant record is final, then removes that wrapper after a later user record
arrives.  The completed assistant record is therefore not token-prefix-stable,
and its old K/V cannot represent the next transcript.  With llama.cpp's stable
ChatML template, the same probe builds a four-turn causal source twice with
identical response hashes, attaches 3,069 of 3,205 source cells, and obtains
byte-identical live-built versus cold-built continuations for sampling seeds
101, 202, and 303.  Model identity alone is consequently insufficient:
agent-history qualification binds the model, tokenizer, chat template, and
engine geometry.  The direct endpoint now runs a four-record prefix-stability
probe at startup and rejects an incompatible template before the first costly
agent prefill.

That control exposed a separate occupancy confound.  With both request slots
empty, two frozen full-transcript prompts produce identical response hashes
under cache-on and cache-off execution.  Replaying the exact physical selected-
record payloads is also exact.  If the native readiness probe first leaves an
unrelated seven-token resource pinned in sequence 0, however, the cache-off
response to prompt 2 changes reproducibly even though sequence 1 is erased,
reports zero cache hits, and receives the same 1,857 prompt tokens.  The changed
hash exactly reproduces the earlier apparent cache mismatch.  This is unified-
KV physical-occupancy sensitivity, not evidence that the selected transcript
or ordinary prefix cache is wrong.  Readiness probes are now ephemeral and
release their resource sequence before measurement.

The first concurrent sparse-session attempt then exposed a related ownership
bug rather than clearing the gate.  The wrapper assigned canonical histories to
distinct source slots, but built them as ordinary prefix-cache requests without
setting llama.cpp's persistent PRA pin.  Under unified-KV scheduling,
\texttt{try\_clear\_idle\_slots()} consequently purged idle source slot 1 while
the other session was active: the slot-3 request attached correctly, whereas
the slot-1 request failed closed with ``source has no encoded context.''  The
fix marks only canonical live-history sources with
\texttt{pra\_pin\_resource}; ordinary no-PRA controls remain unpinned.

After that repair, two Qwen3-14B/ChatML sessions independently built three-turn,
1,262-token live histories in source slots 1 and 3.  Their simultaneous
75\%-budget requests used destination slots 0 and 2, each attached five records and
662 existing K/V cells, re-encoded zero selected-history tokens, and copied no
K/V.  Both complete source trajectories and both sparse target responses were
byte-identical to a separately rebuilt sequential control.  Concurrent target
wall time was 182.9~s versus 296.0~s sequential, but this 1.62$\times$ observed
scheduling ratio is diagnostic only: an unrelated virtual machine and endpoint
security substantially contended for the host during the run.  The result
clears cross-session slot ownership and behavioral-isolation correctness for
this qualified configuration; it is not a sparse-runtime speed claim.

The small-model rows and the task-02 result are mechanism and single-task
correctness gates, not Easy-50 population-level efficacy evidence.  A strict
frozen replay now resubmits the successful task-02 arm's recorded physical
payloads and observations: all 14 assistant responses, including mutation,
verification, and submission turns, reproduce byte-for-byte.  This removes
live filesystem timestamps and other tool nondeterminism from the replay
verdict and clears the single-task frozen-agent gate.  Together with the
qualified Qwen3-14B replication, this clears the current single-task,
multi-seed, cross-model construction, and concurrent-session isolation gates.
The wrapper additionally serializes same-session requests, makes exact
transport retries idempotent, rejects a different generation from the same
logical head as a commit conflict, applies resource deltas transactionally,
and tombstones a session before draining and erasing it.  A direct Qwen3-14B
probe confirms those semantics: an exact retry returns the cached identical
response; two simultaneous requests from one logical parent produce exactly one
HTTP 200 commit and one HTTP 409 conflict; DELETE completes in 4.4~ms after the
winning request, and later traffic receives HTTP 410.  This clears retry,
same-session commit, and completed-request termination.  It does not clear
active cancellation by itself.  To close that gap while preserving the public
non-streaming API, the wrapper consumes llama.cpp's completion stream
internally, retains the live upstream response handle, and closes it when the
session DELETE arrives.  A repeated direct probe observes the upstream stream,
returns HTTP 499 \texttt{generation\_cancelled} to the interrupted request,
returns HTTP 200 to DELETE in 1.54~s, and records llama.cpp cancelling and
releasing the active task.

The eviction/offload gate uses a one-pair Qwen2.5-0.5B configuration so slot
reuse is unavoidable.  The wrapper saves a 24-token canonical sequence as a
295,916-byte llama.cpp slot image, erases both sequence memberships, and the
next pressure session occupies the released source slot.  Restoring the first
session into that same pair, with its PRA pin set atomically by the restore
operation, yields a byte-identical continuation to the never-offloaded control.
No selected text is submitted for prefill, and the checkpoint file is deleted
after successful restore.  This clears explicit disk demotion, capacity reuse,
restore identity, and cleanup for the tested runtime.

That final mechanism gate uses ten alternating-order pairs on the same small
model and fixed synthetic trajectory.  Ordinary full-prefix continuation reads
all 1,495 resident cells; sparse live-K/V attaches 391 cells (26.2\%) across
five causal records, with zero selected-text re-encoding and no K/V copy.  The
one-token target response is identical in every pair.  Median engine prompt
time falls from 174.0 to 161.4~ms (1.08$\times$ ratio of medians; 1.09$\times$
median paired ratio), while endpoint wall time falls from 294.7 to 266.7~ms
(1.10$\times$; 1.11$\times$ paired); sparse wins all ten prompt and wall pairs.
This qualifies an engine-specific sparse runtime benefit at this geometry, not
an Easy-50 task-quality or large-model speedup.  The llama.cpp live-agent path
therefore clears the construction, concurrency, lifecycle, offload, and sparse-
runtime gates.  A campaign-level lock prevents the resumable Easy-50 scheduler
from launching treatment cells before that status is explicitly promoted.  The gate is now
\texttt{QUALIFIED} only for a llama.cpp wrapper with prefix caching enabled and
a configured slot checkpoint directory.  HF, MLX, vLLM, and SGLang now clear
bounded sparse-consumer mechanism gates.  The autonomous agent executions that
previously followed those gates are not current evidence: they predate the
final cache, position, copy-accounting, and Paper~8.5 policy-transfer contract.
Direct MLX now has one fresh post-fix plain/PRA-100/tail-90 task gate: PRA-100
matches all seven plain actions and tail-90 preserves the solve and patch in
five calls.  HF, vLLM, and SGLang still require their own fresh post-fix
plain/PRA-100/PRA-policy executions.  The campaign is therefore frozen, and no
Easy-14 expansion is permitted until one model/task pair per engine passes that
sequence and the reduced-history policy passes Paper~8.5's paired saving,
success, and call-count gate.

\subsubsection{Claim 4: observed coding-agent utility frontier}

Paper~8.5 studies agent-history policies without attributing logical task
effects to an engine.  Its context-qualified Easy-14 campaign uses one
continuous ordinary-role conversation, clean workspaces, Qwen3-Coder-30B at
temperature zero, and a fail-closed 131,072-token context.  The policy receives
no evaluator task identifier, repository label, workspace identity, or explicit
issue boundary.  Persistent \textsc{Full} resolves 10/14 issues in 207 calls and
materializes 8,902,105 cumulative message-content tokens.

Atomic-policy E2 is stopped by the predeclared quality gate after five issues: it
resolves 2/5 where paired \textsc{Full} resolves 4/5, uses 84 versus 85 calls,
and materializes 1,016,037 versus 1,415,568 tokens.  The apparent 28.22\% raw
paired saving becomes zero under failure-aware accounting.  Atomic-policy E3 is
stopped even earlier, before its first deletion, because the nominally
full-materialization replay has already diverged: 2/4 resolutions and 126 calls
versus 3/4 and 79 for \textsc{Full}.  These stopped prefixes are evidence about
quality and backend repeatability, not incomplete rows silently projected to
14 tasks.

The causal effect of selection is isolated on Task~5 by forking from the exact
saved four-episode \textsc{Full} prefix and a clean workspace, with two repeats
per arm.  \textsc{Full}, E3, and E2 each resolve 1/2.  E3 materializes 219,442
tokens against a 298,777-token same-trajectory full-history counterfactual
(26.55\% gross, 16.62\% paired saving) and adds one aggregate call.  E2
materializes 544,181 against 937,220 counterfactual tokens (41.94\% gross), but
its 27-call trajectory costs 106.77\% more than the eight-call \textsc{Full}
control.  Even byte-identical first \textsc{Full} inputs produce different
first actions and opposite official outcomes across repeats, so repeatability
is measured explicitly and model-visible equality is keyed by the selected-
message digest rather than the pre-selection request digest.

A subsequent stricter cohort removes that pairing ambiguity by requiring an
officially successful \textsc{Full} execution from the identical saved prefix
before each treatment.  M2/P1 resolves Tasks~3--5 in 7, 6, and 9 calls,
respectively, versus 7, 14, and 9 for the controls.  Along the candidate's own
trajectories it materializes 239,881 of 403,340 full-counterfactual tokens, a
40.53\% logical saving, while preserving 3/3 resolutions and reducing calls
from 30 to 22.  Relative to the controls' realized 556,072 tokens, total-work
saving is 56.86\%; that larger number includes the shorter Task~4 trajectory
and is not a pure selector effect.  The result passes the predeclared small-
cohort promotion gate but does not establish a population accuracy rate or a
production default.  Task~6 was withheld after two independent \textsc{Full}
controls both failed, rather than being misattributed to selection.

\centering
\small

is paired cumulative input over the realized trajectory; gross per-request
reduction alone cannot qualify a profile.}

\toprule
Product label & Frozen logical policy & Current evidence & Decision \\
\midrule

Qualified correctness control \\
